where the first map
\pi^{\prime}_{1}
is the left vertical map in (
3.10.1
) and the second map
\pi^{\prime}_{2}
is the composition
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r,\varepsilon]}\mathcal{N}^{[r]}_{n+1}
. Explicitly, fix an
O_{F}
-linear isogeny

(3.10.3)

\alpha\colon\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}\mathbb{X}\times{\overline{\mathbb{E}}},

such that
\ker\alpha\subset\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}[\varpi]
and
\alpha^{*}(\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}\times\varpi^{\varepsilon}\lambda_{{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}})=\lambda_{\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}}
. Then the space
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is the closed formal sublocus of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}^{[r]}_{n+1}
parametrizing tuples

(X,\iota_{X},\lambda_{X},\rho_{X},Y^{\prime},\iota_{Y^{\prime}},\lambda_{Y^{\prime}},\rho_{Y^{\prime}})

such that
(\rho_{X}\times\rho_{\bar{}\mathcal{E}_{S}})^{-1}\circ\alpha\circ\rho_{Y^{\prime}}
lifts to an isogeny
\tilde{\alpha}:Y^{\prime}X\times\bar{}\mathcal{E}
.

Let us discuss the geometry of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
. We distinguish the case where
r
is even from the case where
r
is odd.

Let
r
be even. If
r\leq n
, there is a closed embedding
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r]}\hookrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
, sending
(YX)
to
(X,Y\times\bar{}\mathcal{E})
(we are dropping all auxiliary structure from the notation). Let
{u}\in\Hom^{\circ}_{O_{F}}(\mathbb{Y}^{\prime},{\overline{\mathbb{E}}})
be the projection of
\alpha
in (
3.10.3
) to the second factor. By taking duals and using the polarization, we may identify
\Hom^{\circ}_{O_{F}}(\mathbb{Y}^{\prime},{\overline{\mathbb{E}}})
with
\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
. In this way, we obtain the special vector
u
of length one in

\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
. Recall the
\mathcal{Z}
-divisor and the
\mathcal{Y}
-divisor
\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}
on
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]}
, comp.
[
30
, §5.2]
. They are both relative Cartier divisors. Furthermore,
\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}
is a regular Cartier divisor isomorphic to
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[r]}
, cf.
[
30
, Prop. 5.20]
). If
r=0
, then
\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}=\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}
. If
r\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}
is a reducible Cartier divisor, as follows from the non-emptiness of the difference divisor
\mathcal{D}(u)=\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}\setminus\mathcal{Y}(u/\varpi)^{[r]}
(this can be seen for
n=2
from the theory of quasi-canonical divisors
[
10
]
, and for
n\geq 3
from the non-emptiness of the underlying reduced scheme, as can be checked using the Bruhat–Tits stratification in
[
3
]
and
[
30
]
). We obtain a cartesian diagram

(3.10.4)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.28477pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.35304pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.35306pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.39708pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.20145pt\raise-16.68945pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.60417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\square}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 50.39708pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.6529pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-17.28477pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.28477pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.28477pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.28477pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Here the right downward arrow associates to
(Y^{\prime}X\times\bar{}\mathcal{E})
the second projection
(Y^{\prime}\bar{}\mathcal{E})
(this is the analogue of
\pi_{2}
in (
3.8.1
) for
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
instead of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{[r]}_{n}}
). The left downward arrow associates to
(YX)
the map
(\bar{}\mathcal{E}Y\times\bar{}\mathcal{E})
. It follows that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is formally reducible when
r\geq 2
, hence
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is not regular for
r\geq 2
, i.e., there is no analogue of Proposition
3.5.2
for
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
.

Write
\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}
as the sum of effective Cartier divisors

\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r]}=\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r],+}+\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r],-},

where
\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r],+}=\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}
. Correspondingly, we obtain by pullback of
\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r],+}=\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}
, resp. of
\mathcal{Y}(u)^{[r],-}
under the map
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\mathcal{Z}(u)^{[r]}
the closed formal subschemes
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r],+}=\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r]}
, resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r],-}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r],+}
is regular and its generic fiber is the member of the RZ-tower of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
corresponding to the parahoric subgroup
K_{n}^{[r],+}
of §
10.1
. It is conceivable that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r],-}
is also regular and that its generic fiber is the member of the RZ-tower of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
corresponding to the parahoric subgroup
K_{n}^{[r],-}
of §
10.1
.

Now let
r
be even and
r=n+1
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{[n+1]}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[n]}
making the following diagram commutative,

(3.10.5)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.40831pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.82587pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{[n+1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.62076pt\raise 4.72855pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.72818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.40831pt\raise-16.68945pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.3978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}^{\prime}\times\pi^{\prime}_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.72818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[n]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.49153pt\raise-16.68945pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.66667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.49153pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-28.94397pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[n+1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.62076pt\raise-28.65036pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72855pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\simeq}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.94397pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.94397pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[0]}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Here the isomorphisms in the bottom line

(3.10.6)

\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n]}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]},\text{ resp. }\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[n+1]}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[0]}

are the
rescaling isomorphisms
which send e.g.
(X,\iota,\lambda)\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n]}
to
(X,\iota,\lambda_{0})\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
, where
\lambda=\varpi\lambda_{0}
. The isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{[n+1]}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[n]}
sends
(Y^{\prime}X\times\bar{}\mathcal{E})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{[n+1]}
to
(Y\times\bar{}\mathcal{E}X^{\prime})
, where
X^{\prime}
is the dual of the rescaled module of
Y^{\prime}
, and
Y
is the dual of the rescaled module
X
.

Now let
r
be odd. When
r=1
(hence
\varepsilon=1
) then, trivially,
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r,\varepsilon]}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[1]}
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
. In general

(3.10.7)

{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r-1]},

such that
\pi^{\prime}_{1}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
coincides with the natural projection
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r-1]}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
. The isomorphism is given by sending
(Y^{\prime}X\times\bar{}\mathcal{E})\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
to the element
(YX)\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0,r-1]}
, where
Y\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[r-1]}
is the dual of
(Y^{\prime})^{\vee}/\im(\bar{}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}(Y^{\prime})^{\vee})
, and where the isogeny
YX
is the dual of the composition
X^{\vee}(Y^{\prime})^{\vee}Y^{\vee}
. Note that, by Proposition
3.5.2
, the isomorphism (
3.10.7
) may also be written as
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[r-1]}
, in analogy with the isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{[n+1]}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[n]}
for
n
odd, occurring in (
3.10.5
).

However, for
r
even with
r\leq n
, there is no identification of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
with an
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n}
-space. Maybe there is a better chance with the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r],-}
introduced above.

In conclusion, when
r
is odd, then
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is regular with semi-stable reduction and the generic fiber of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is a member of the RZ-tower associated to
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
(in fact corresponding to the parahoric subgroup
K_{n}^{[r]}
, cf. §
10.1
).

3.11.
Arithmetic intersection number for AT of type
(0,r)

The arithmetic intersection number in this context is defined as

(3.11.1)

\left\langle{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}},g{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]}}:=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]},{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\cap^{\mathbb{L}}g{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}),\quad g\in G_{W_{\epsilon(r)+1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}.

Here we take the convention that
W_{\epsilon(r)+1}=W_{0}
when
\epsilon(r)=1
.
Note that, by (
3.10.5
), the intersection number (
3.11.1
), for
r=n+1
even, coincides with the intersection number (
3.6.1
) for
r=n
.
In the special case
r=1
(hence
\varepsilon=1
), we have
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r,\varepsilon]}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[1]}
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
; in this case,
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{[r]}_{n}}
is the graph of the closed embedding
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[1]}
, and this intersection number has been considered in
[
19
]
, where an AT statement is proved. See §
12
for more details.

In the case when
r
is even and
r\leq n
, there are the following variants of (
3.11.1
),

(3.11.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+},g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]}}

\displaystyle:=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]},\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+}\cap^{\mathbb{L}}g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+}),

\displaystyle\left\langle\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-},g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]}}

\displaystyle:=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r],-},\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-}\cap^{\mathbb{L}}g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-}),

\displaystyle\left\langle\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+},g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]}}

\displaystyle:=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}^{[r]},\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],+}\cap^{\mathbb{L}}g\widetilde{}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{[r],-}),

where
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
.

4.
The analytic side

4.1.
Groups

We recall the group-theoretic setup of
[
18
, §2]
in both homogeneous and inhomogeneous settings. Let
n\geq 1
. In the homogeneous setting, set

(4.1.1)

G^{\prime}:=\Res_{F/F_{0}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}),

a reductive algebraic group over
F_{0}
. Let
W
be a
F/F_{0}
-hermitian space of dimension
n+1
. Fix
u\in W
a non-isotropic vector (the
special vector
), and let
W^{\flat}=\langle u\rangle^{\perp}
. Set

(4.1.2)

G_{W}=\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})\times\mathrm{U}(W),

a reductive algebraic group over
F_{0}
. We have the notion of a
regular semi-simple element
, for
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})
and for
g\in G_{W}(F_{0})
and the notion of
matching

\gamma\leftrightarrow g
, cf.
[
18
, §2]
. The notions of regular semi-simple elements are with respect to the actions of reductive algebraic groups over
F_{0}
, namely
H^{\prime}_{1,2}=H_{1}^{\prime}\times H_{2}^{\prime}:=\Res_{F/F_{0}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1})

on
G^{\prime}
, resp.
H_{W}:=\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})\times\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})
on
G_{W}
.
It is important to note that the first action is arranged
after
the choice of the special vector
u\in W
. The sets of regular semi-simple elements are denoted by
G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
and
G_{W}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
respectively.
Let
W_{0}
,
W_{1}
be the two isomorphism classes of
F/F_{0}
-hermitian spaces of dimension
n+1
. Note that, unlike the convention in
[
18
]
, for the moment we do not assume
W_{0}
to be split. Take the special vectors
u_{0}\in W_{0}
and
u_{1}\in W_{1}
to have the same norm (not necessarily a unit). Then we have a bijection of regular semisimple orbits,

(4.1.3)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 61.85007pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-61.85007pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl[G_{W_{0}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]\bigsqcup\,\bigl[G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.72507pt\raise 4.34428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.85007pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.85007pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigr[G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Remark 4.1.1
.

In our previous work, the norm of the special vector is assumed to be one. In that case we characterize the bijection of orbits in terms of invariant theory. If the norm of the special vector is not equal to one, we need to scale the Hermitian form so that the norm of the special vector becomes one and then we can apply the invariant-theoretical characterization of the orbit comparison.

In the inhomogeneous setting, recall the symmetric space

(4.1.4)

S_{n+1}=\{\gamma\in\Res_{F/F_{0}}\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\mid\gamma\overline{\gamma}=1_{n+1}\}.

Here
\gamma\overline{\gamma}
is the natural involution on
\Res_{F/F_{0}}\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
induced by the Galois conjugation relative to
F/F_{0}
. There is the map

(4.1.5)

\mathfrak{r}:\Res_{F/F_{0}}\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}S_{n+1},\quad\gamma\gamma\overline{\gamma}^{-1},

which induces an isomorphism
(\Res_{F/F_{0}}\mathrm{GL}_{n+1})/\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\simeq S_{n+1}.
We have the notion of a
regular semi-simple element
, for
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})
and for
g\in\mathrm{U}(W)(F_{0})
and the notion of
matching

\gamma\leftrightarrow g
. Here
W
is any hermitian space of dimension
n+1
and the notions of regular semi-simple elements are with respect to the conjugation actions of
H^{\prime}:=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
on
S
, resp., of
H:=\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})
on
\mathrm{U}(W)
. The sets of regular semi-simple elements are denoted by
S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
and
\mathrm{U}(W)(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
respectively. The inhomogeneous version of the bijection (
4.1.3
) is

(4.1.6)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 71.5848pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-71.5848pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl[\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]\bigsqcup\,\bigl[\mathrm{U}(W_{1})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.4598pt\raise 4.34428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 95.5848pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 95.5848pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigr[S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}\bigr]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We use the same notation for the map

\mathfrak{r}:\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)S_{n+1}(F_{0})
introduced in (
4.1.5
) and the map

(4.1.7)

\mathfrak{r}:G^{\prime}(F_{0})S_{n+1}(F_{0}),

obtained by precomposing
\mathfrak{r}
with
G^{\prime}(F_{0})\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)
given by
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2}
. Then
G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}=\mathfrak{r}^{-1}(S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}})
, and
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matches
g=(g_{1},g_{2})\in G_{W_{i}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matches
g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}\in\mathrm{U}(W_{i})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
.

We will also need the semi-Lie version of this set-up in the inhomogeneous version. Set

(4.1.8)

W^{\prime}=W^{\prime}_{n+1}=F_{0}^{n+1}\times(F_{0}^{n+1})^{*}.

There is the notion of a regular semi-simple element, for
(\gamma,v)\in(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})(F_{0})
and for
(g,u)\in(\mathrm{U}(W)\times W)(F_{0})
, cf.
[
29
, §2]
. These notions are with respect to group actions of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, resp.
\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})
. Again, there is a notion of matching between elements of
(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})(F_{0})_{\rm rs}
and
(\mathrm{U}(W)\times W)(F_{0})_{\rm rs}
.

4.2.
Orbital integrals

We recall the orbital integrals in both homogeneous and inhomogeneous settings, following
[
18
, §5]
.
Now we assume that
F/F_{0}
is an
unramified
extension of non-archimedean local fields. We then have a unique extension of
\eta
to an unramified quadratic character
\widetilde{\eta}:F^{\times}\{\pm 1\}
. We also define the twist

\widetilde{\eta}_{s}(z)=\widetilde{\eta}(z)|z|_{F}^{s/2},\quad z\in F^{\times},

for a complex parameter
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

We first introduce a transfer factor on
G^{\prime}
and
S_{n+1}
respectively. For
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, we define

(4.2.1)

\Delta^{+}(\gamma)=\det((\gamma^{i}e_{n+1})_{i=0}^{n}),

where

(4.2.2)

e_{n+1}=\,^{t}(0,\cdots,0,1)\in M_{(n+1)\times 1}(F_{0}).

For
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we define
the transfer factor on the symmetric space
S_{n+1}
as
1
1

1

Here our transfer
factor, when specialized to
s=0
, coincides with
[
19
, §2.4]
and
[
29
, §2.3]
where
\widetilde{\eta}(\det(\gamma))=1
by our choice of
\widetilde{\eta}
.

(4.2.3)

\omega_{S,s}(\gamma)=\widetilde{\eta}_{-s}(\Delta^{+}(\gamma)),\quad\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}.

It satisfies

\omega_{S,s}(h^{-1}\gamma h)=\eta_{s}(h)\omega_{S,s}(\gamma),\quad h\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0}).

Here we write
\eta_{s}(h)
for
\eta_{s}(\det h)
.

In the homogeneous case, we define for
\gamma=(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{n+1})\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
,
the transfer factor on the group
G^{\prime}
as

(4.2.4)

\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma)=\widetilde{\eta}^{n}(\gamma_{n}^{-1}\gamma_{n+1})|\gamma_{n}|_{F}^{-s}\,\omega_{S,2s}(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)).

Then it is easy to verify

\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(h_{1}^{-1}\gamma h_{2})=|\det h_{1}|_{F}^{s}\eta(h_{2})\,\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma),\quad(h_{1},h_{2})\in(H_{1}^{\prime}\times H_{2}^{\prime})(F_{0}).

Here we denote

\eta(h_{2}):=\eta(\det h_{2}^{\prime})^{n-1}\eta(\det h_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{n}\quad\text{for}\quad h_{2}=(h_{2}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in H_{2}^{\prime}({F_{0}})=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0}).

Next we introduce some
weighted orbital integrals
. In the homogeneous setting, for an element
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, for a function
\varphi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(G^{\prime})
and for a complex parameter
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we define
2
2

2

Note that, contrary to e.g.
[
19
]
and
[
29
]
, we incorporate the transfer factor in the definition of the weighted orbital integral.

(4.2.5)

\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime},s):=\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma)\int_{H_{1,2}^{\prime}(F_{0})}\varphi^{\prime}(h_{1}^{-1}\gamma h_{2})\lvert\det h_{1}\rvert_{F}^{s}\eta(h_{2})\,\mathrm{d}h_{1}\,\mathrm{d}h_{2},

where we use fixed Haar measures on
H_{1}^{\prime}(F_{0})
and
H_{2}^{\prime}(F_{0})
and the product Haar measure on
H_{1,2}^{\prime}(F_{0})=H_{1}^{\prime}(F_{0})\times H_{2}^{\prime}(F_{0})
.
We further define the value and derivative at
s=0
,

(4.2.6)

\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}):=\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime},0)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}):=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\Orb({\gamma},\varphi^{\prime},s).

The integral defining
\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime},s)
is absolutely convergent, and
\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime})
depend only on the orbit of
\gamma
.

Now we turn to the inhomogeneous setting. For
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, for a function
\phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
, and for a complex parameter
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we introduce the
weighted orbital integral

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime},s):=\omega_{S,s}(\gamma)\int_{H^{\prime}(F_{0})}\phi^{\prime}(h^{-1}\gamma h)\lvert\det h\rvert^{s}\eta(h)\,dh,

as well as the value and derivative at
s=0
,

(4.2.8)

\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}):=\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime},0)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}):=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime},s).

As in the homogeneous setting, the integral defining
\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime},s)
is absolutely convergent, and
\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\gamma,\phi^{\prime})
depend only on the orbit of
\gamma
.

Now let us define orbital integrals on the unitary side. In the homogeneous setting, for
W
the
(n+1)
-dimensional hermitian space as above, for an element
g\in G_{W}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, and for a function
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W})
, we define the orbital integral

(4.2.9)

\Orb(g,f):=\int_{H_{W}(F_{0})}f(h_{1}^{-1}gh_{2})\,dh_{1}\,dh_{2}.

Here the Haar measure on
H_{W}(F_{0})=H(F_{0})\times H(F_{0})
is the product of two identical Haar measures on
H(F_{0})
.

In the inhomogeneous setting, let
G=\mathrm{U}(W)
. We define for
g\in G(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
,

(4.2.10)

\Orb(g,f):=\int_{H(F_{0})}f(h^{-1}gh)\,dh,

where we use the same fixed Haar measure on
H(F_{0})
.

We also have use for the semi-Lie versions of these orbital integrals. For
(\gamma,w^{\prime})\in(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
and
\Phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
, we set

(4.2.11)

\Orb((\gamma,w^{\prime}),\Phi^{\prime},s):=\omega_{S\times W^{\prime},s}(\gamma,w^{\prime})\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}\Phi^{\prime}(h\cdot(\gamma,w^{\prime}))|\det(h)|^{s}\eta(h)\,dh,

and obtain
\Orb((\gamma,w^{\prime}),\Phi^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\partial Orb}((\gamma,w^{\prime}),\Phi^{\prime})
as before. Here the transfer factor
\omega_{S\times W^{\prime},s}(\gamma,w^{\prime})
is defined
similarly to (
4.2.3
),

(4.2.12)

\omega_{S\times W^{\prime},s}((\gamma,w^{\prime}),s)=\widetilde{\eta}_{-s}(\Delta^{+}(\gamma,w^{\prime})),\quad(\gamma,w^{\prime})\in(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}},

where
\Delta^{+}(\gamma,w^{\prime})
is defined similarly to
(
4.2.1
): for
w^{\prime}=(e,e^{*})\in W^{\prime}
,

(4.2.13)

\Delta^{+}(\gamma,w^{\prime})=\det((\gamma^{i}e)_{i=0}^{n}).

The value of
\omega_{S\times W^{\prime},s}
at
s=0
recovers
[
29
, eq. (2.17)]
. On the unitary side, we have

(4.2.14)

\Orb((g,v),\Phi)=\int_{H(F_{0})}\Phi(h\cdot(g,v))\,dh,,\quad\Phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W)\times W).

Having fixed the transfer factors, we have the notion of
transfer
between functions
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
and pairs of functions
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
(
[
19
, Def. 2.2]
), and between functions
\phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
and pairs of functions
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1}))
(
[
19
, Def. 2.4]
) and between functions
\Phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})
and pairs of functions
(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0})\times W_{0})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1})\times W_{1})
. We recall here the notion of the transfer only in the homogeneous group setting:
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
and
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
are called transfers of each other if, for all regular semisimple
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})
, we have

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\Orb(g,f_{0}),\quad\text{if $\gamma$ matches an element $g\in G_{W_{0}}(F_{0})$},\\
\Orb(g,f_{1}),\quad\text{if $\gamma$ matches an element $g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})$}.\end{cases}

The other cases are defined similarly.

Definition 4.2.1
.

We say two functions
\varphi^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
(resp.
\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi^{\prime}_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
) are
equivalent
, denoted by
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\sim\varphi^{\prime}_{2}
(resp.
\phi^{\prime}_{1}\sim\phi^{\prime}_{2}
), if

\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}_{1})=\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}_{2}),\quad\text{(resp. $\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{1})=\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{2})$)}

for all
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
(resp.
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
).

Let us relate functions on
G^{\prime}(F_{0})
and on
S_{n+1}(F_{0})
. For
\varphi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G^{\prime})
, define the function
\varphi^{\prime\natural}
on
S_{n+1}(F_{0})
by

(4.2.16)

\varphi^{\prime\natural}(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)):=\widetilde{\eta}^{n}(\gamma)\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})}\varphi^{\prime}(h^{-1},h^{-1}\gamma h_{2})\eta^{n}(h_{2})\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}h_{2},\quad\gamma\in\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F).

It is easy to see that the right hand side depends only on
\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)
rather than on
\gamma
, hence indeed defines a function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
.
We also define a family version: for
s\in\mathbb{C}
,

(4.2.17)

\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s}(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)):=\widetilde{\eta}^{n}(\gamma)\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})}\varphi^{\prime}(h^{-1},h^{-1}\gamma h_{2})|\det h|_{F}^{s}\eta^{n}(h_{2})\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}h_{2}.

Then

\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s}(\gamma)|_{s=0}=\varphi^{\prime\natural}(\gamma),\quad\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0}).

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G^{\prime})
and
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
. Then

\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime},s)=\Orb(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma),\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s},2s).

Proof.

By definition (
4.2.5
), for
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, we have

\Orb\bigl((\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}),\varphi^{\prime},s\bigr)=\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma)\int_{H_{1,2}^{\prime}(F_{0})}\varphi^{\prime}(h^{-1}\gamma_{1}h_{1},h^{-1}\gamma_{2}h_{2})\lvert\det h\rvert_{F}^{s}\eta(h_{1})\eta^{n-1}(h_{1}h_{2})\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}h_{1}\,\mathrm{d}h_{2},

where
h\in H_{1}^{\prime}(F_{0})=\mathrm{GL}_{n-1}(F)
and
(h_{1},h_{2})\in H_{2}^{\prime}(F_{0})=\mathrm{GL}_{n-1}(F_{0})\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
. Replacing
h
by
\gamma_{1}h_{1}h
, we have

\Orb\bigl((\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}),\varphi^{\prime},s\bigr)=\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma)|\gamma_{1}|_{F}^{s}\int_{H_{1,2}^{\prime}(F_{0})}\varphi^{\prime}\bigl(h^{-1},h^{-1}h_{1}^{-1}(\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2})h_{2}\bigr)\lvert\det hh_{1}\rvert_{F}^{s}\eta(h_{1})\eta^{n-1}(h_{1}h_{2})\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}h_{1}\,\mathrm{d}h_{2}.

Comparing with the definition (
4.2.17
) of
\varphi^{\prime\natural}
, we have

\Orb\bigl((\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}),\varphi^{\prime},s\bigr)=\omega_{G^{\prime},s}(\gamma)|\gamma_{1}|_{F}^{s}\widetilde{\eta}^{-(n-1)}(\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2})\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n-1}(F_{)}}\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s}(h_{1}^{-1}\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)h_{1})\lvert\det h_{1}\rvert_{F}^{s}\eta(h_{1})\,\mathrm{d}h_{1}.

By the definition of the transfer factor on
G^{\prime}
(
4.2.4
) and
\lvert\det h_{1}\rvert_{F}=\lvert\det h_{1}\rvert^{2}_{F_{0}}
, the last equation is equal to

\omega_{S,2s}(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma))\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n-1}(F_{)}}\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s}(h_{1}^{-1}\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)h_{1})\lvert\det h_{1}\rvert^{2s}\eta(h_{1})\,\mathrm{d}h_{1},

which is equal to
\Orb(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma),\varphi^{\prime\natural}_{s},2s)
by (
4.2.7
).
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

This lemma is a more general version of
[
14
, Lem. 3.7.2]
(note that in
loc. cit
the orbital integral is not normalized by the transfer factor).

Corollary 4.2.4
.

We have

\Orb(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime})=\Orb(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma),\varphi^{\prime\natural}),\quad\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime})=2\operatorname{\partial Orb}(\mathfrak{r}(\gamma),\varphi^{\prime\natural}),\quad\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}.

∎

On the unitary side, for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W})
, we define a function
f^{\natural}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W))
,

(4.2.18)

\displaystyle f^{\natural}(g)=\int_{U(W^{\flat})}f(h,hg)\,dh,\quad g\in\mathrm{U}(W)(F_{0}).

Then it is easy to see that

\Orb((g_{1},g_{2}),f)=\Orb(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2},f^{\natural}),\quad(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat})(F_{0})\times\mathrm{U}(W)(F_{0}).

From Corollary
4.2.4
we therefore deduce the following statement.

Corollary 4.2.5
.

The function
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
is a transfer of
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
if and only if
\varphi^{\prime\natural}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
is a transfer of
(f^{\natural}_{0},f^{\natural}_{1})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1}))
.∎

Proof.

This follows from the definition of the transfer in the two settings
[
19
, Def. 2.2 and Def. 2.4]
(cf. the definition (
4.2.15
) for the homogeneous group setting).
∎

5.
FL and AFL

From now on and for the rest of the paper, we let
W_{0}
, resp.
W_{1}
, be the split, resp. non-split, hermitian space of dimension
n+1
. In this section, we recall the FL and the AFL. We do this to facilitate the comparison with the quasi-canonical FL/AFL proved below.

5.1.
The FL

To state the FL, resp. the AFL, we assume that the special vectors
u_{0}\in W_{0}
, resp.
u_{1}\in W_{1}
, have unit length. Let
K_{0}\subset\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0})
be the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup which is the stabilizer of a selfdual lattice
\Lambda_{0}
containing
u_{0}
and, similarly, let
K^{\flat}_{0}\subset\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})(F_{0})
be the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup which is the stabilizer of the selfdual lattice
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}=\Lambda_{0}\cap W_{0}^{\flat}
, i.e.,
K_{0}^{\flat}=K_{0}\cap\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})(F_{0})
.
Throughout the paper, the Haar measures on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
are normalized such that
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F_{0}})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})
have measure one. For the FL, we furthermore choose the Haar measures on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
and on
\mathrm{U}(W_{0})
such that
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F})
and
K_{0}^{\flat}
have volume one (this is independent of the choice of
\Lambda_{0}
). The Fundamental Lemma of Jacquet–Rallis is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1.1
.

Let
p>2
.

(a)

(Inhomogeneous group version)
The characteristic function
\mathbf{1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n})
transfers to the pair of functions
(\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1}))
.

(b)

(Homogeneous group version)
The characteristic function
\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F})\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
transfers to the pair of functions
(\mathbf{1}_{K^{\flat}_{0}\times K_{0}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
.

(c)

(Semi-Lie algebra version)
The characteristic function
\mathbf{1}_{(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime}_{n+1})(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime}_{n+1})
transfers to the pair of functions
(\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}\times\Lambda_{0}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0})\times W_{0})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1})\times W_{1})
.

The equal characteristic analog of the Jacquet–Rallis FL conjecture was proved by Z. Yun for
p>n
, cf.
[
26
]
; J. Gordon deduced the
p
-adic case for
p
large, but unspecified, cf.
[
26
, App.]
. The general statement above has two proofs: a purely local proof is given by Beuzart-Plessis
[
1
]
, another one essentially of a global nature is given by the third author
[
29
]
when
p>n
and in general by Z. Zhang
[
30
]
. There is also a Lie algebra version of FL, which is also known for any residue characteristic.

5.2.
The AFL

Next we recall the AFL. We identify
W_{1}
with
\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
defined in §
3.2
and choose the special vector
u_{1}\in W_{1}
to be
{u}\in\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
defined in §
3.8
, assumed to have length
1
. Then we may identify the hermitian space
W_{1}^{\flat}
with
\mathbb{V}_{n}
. Recall that
\mathcal{Z}(u)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
is the special divisor on
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
associated to
u
. It may be identified with the inclusion of RZ-spaces
\mathcal{N}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
, cf.
[
11
, §5]
. Then
G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})
acts on
\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
via this identification and hence the arithmetic intersection number
\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),g\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}
defined in §
3.9
makes sense for
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})
.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Let
p>2
.

(a)

(Inhomogeneous group version)

Suppose that
\gamma\in S_{n+1}({F_{0}})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matches an element
g\in\mathrm{U}(W_{1})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
. Then

\bigl\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),(1\times g)\mathcal{Z}(u)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}\cdot\log q=-\operatorname{\partial Orb}\bigl(\gamma,\mathbf{1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\bigr).

(b)

(Homogeneous group version)

Suppose that
\gamma\in G^{\prime}({F_{0}})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matches an element
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
. Then

\bigl\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),g\mathcal{Z}(u)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}\cdot\log q=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\partial Orb}\bigl(\gamma,\mathbf{1}_{G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})}\bigr).

(c)

(Semi-Lie algebra version)

Suppose that
(\gamma,u^{\prime})\in(S_{n+1}\times W_{n+1}^{\prime})({F_{0}})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matches an element
(g,u)\in(\mathrm{U}(W_{1})\times W_{1})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, where
u=u_{1}\in W_{1}
is the fixed special vector. Then

\bigl\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),(1\times g)\mathcal{Z}(u)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n+1}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}\cdot\log q=-\operatorname{\partial Orb}\bigl((\gamma,u^{\prime}),\mathbf{1}_{(S_{n+1}\times W_{n+1}^{\prime})(O_{F_{0}})}\bigr).

Here
\bigl\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),(1\times g)\mathcal{Z}(u)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{N}_{n+1}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n+1}\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1},\mathcal{Z}(u)\cap^{\mathbb{L}}(1\times g)\mathcal{Z}(u))
.

This is proved by the third author
[
29
]
when
F_{0}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
p>n
, for a general
F_{0}
with residue cardinality
q>n
by
Mihatsch and the third author in
[
17
]
, and finally in full generality by Z. Zhang
[
30
]
. All of these works are essentially of global nature, relying on the modularity of generating series of special divisors.

6.
Transfer theorems

In this section, we will formulate and prove some transfer theorems which arise in connection with the quasi-canonical fundamental lemma and some variants of it.
We continue to denote by
W_{0}
(resp.
W_{1}
) a split (resp. non-split) Hermitian space of dimension
n+1
.

6.1.
Open compact subgroups

Let
n\geq 1
and
0\leq r\leq n
. We first define open compact subgroups, on the unitary group side and on the general linear group side, in a generality that is greater than is required for the quasi-canonical FL, but will be needed later.

Recall the parity
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(r)\in\{0,1\}
of
r
, defined in (
3.5.1
). Fix a special vector
u_{0}
of norm
\varpi^{\varepsilon}
in
W_{0}
. We also fix a lattice
\Lambda_{0}\in{\mathrm{Vert}}^{0}(W_{0})
with
u_{0}\in\Lambda_{0}
. Let
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}
be the lattice
W^{\flat}_{0}\cap\Lambda_{0}
. It is a vertex lattice of type
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
(this can be seen using
[
15
, Lem. 7.2.2]
), i.e., selfdual when
\varepsilon=0
, resp. almost selfdual when
\varepsilon=1
. We also fix a lattice
\Lambda^{\flat}\in{\mathrm{Vert}}^{r}(W_{0}^{\flat})
such that
\Lambda^{\flat}\subset\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}
or, equivalently,

(6.1.1)

\Lambda^{\flat}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle\subseteq\Lambda_{0}.

The following lemma shows the independence of our results of the choices made.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

The group
\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})
acts transitively on the set of
(\Lambda^{\flat},\Lambda_{0})
satisfying (
6.1.1
).

Proof.

We can assume
\Lambda^{\flat}
is the standard vertex lattice of type
r
(they are all conjugate under
\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})
). Consider the
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
-hermitian space
\overline{W}=(\Lambda^{\flat}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle)^{\vee}/(\Lambda^{\flat}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle)
of dimension
r+\varepsilon
. Then the set of self-dual
\Lambda_{0}
containing
\Lambda^{\flat}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle
corresponds to the set of lagrangian subspaces of
\overline{W}
. Hence we need to see that the subgroup in the finite unitary group
\mathrm{U}(\overline{W})
fixing the elements of the subspace
\langle\overline{u}_{0}\rangle^{\vee}/\langle\overline{u}_{0}\rangle\subset\overline{W}
acts transitively on the set of all lagrangian subspaces of
\overline{W}
. There are two cases. Either the subspace is zero, in which case the subgroup is the whole unitary group
\mathrm{U}(\overline{W})
. Then it is well known by Witt’s theorem that
\mathrm{U}(\overline{W})
acts transitively on the set of all lagrangian subspaces of
\overline{W}
. In the alternative case the subspace is a non-isotropic line in
\overline{W}
. In this case this again follows from Witt’s theorem which implies that
\mathrm{U}(\overline{W})
acts transitively on the set of vectors
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\frac{r+\varepsilon}{2}},u)
of
\overline{W}
such that
w_{1},\ldots,w_{\frac{r+\varepsilon}{2}}
are a basis of a lagrangian subspace and
u
is a non-isotropic vector of given length.
∎

Define open compact subgroups

(6.1.2)

K_{n+1}=K_{n+1}^{[0]}:=\mathrm{U}(\Lambda_{0})\subseteq\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0}),\quad K_{n}^{[r]}:=\mathrm{U}(\Lambda^{\flat})\subseteq\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})(F_{0}).

We define a finite index subgroup of
K_{n}^{[r]}
by

(6.1.3)

\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}:=K_{n}^{[r]}\cap\mathrm{U}(\Lambda_{0})=K_{n}^{[r]}\cap K_{n+1}.

Note that when
r
is odd,
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}
is not a parahoric subgroup when
n>1
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

(i) When
r=0
, then
K_{n}^{[0]}=\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[0]}
is a hyperspecial subgroup.

(ii) When
r
is even, then
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle
. Then
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}=\mathrm{U}(\Lambda^{\flat})\cap\mathrm{U}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})=K_{n}^{[r,0]}
is a parahoric subgroup, the joint stabilizer of a vertex lattice of type
0
and a vertex lattice of type
r
contained in it.

(iii) When
r=1
, we have
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}=\ker({K}_{n}^{[1]}\mathbb{F}^{1}_{q^{2}})
, where the homomorphism is given by

{K}_{n}^{[1]}\mathrm{U}((\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})^{\vee}/\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})=\ker(\Nm\colon\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{\times}\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})=:\mathbb{F}^{1}_{q^{2}}.

In particular,
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}
is a normal subgroup of
{K}_{n}^{[1]}
(but
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}
is not a normal subgroup of
{K}_{n}^{[r]}
for general
r
). To see one inclusion, note that an element in the kernel of this homomorphism induces the identity automorphism on the two-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
-hermitian space
(\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee}/\Lambda^{\flat})\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle^{\vee}/\langle u_{0}\rangle
. It therefore fixes the line in this space defined by
\Lambda_{0}
and hence lies in
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}
. Conversely, an element of
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}
respects the decomposition into the two hermitian subspaces and induces the identity on the second summand. Since it also fixes an isotropic line in
(\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee}/\Lambda^{\flat})\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle^{\vee}/\langle u_{0}\rangle
, it induces the identity automorphism, and thus lies in the kernel.

Remark 6.1.3
.

Recall that the members of the RZ-tower of rigid-analytic spaces corresponding to the local Shimura datum
(\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0}),(1,0,\ldots,0),b_{\rm basic})
are parametrized by open compact subgroups
K\subset\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})(F_{0})
, cf.
[
21
, §5]
. For instance, the generic fiber of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[r]}
corresponds to
{K}_{n}^{[r]}
, cf.
[
21
, §4.25]
. The generic fiber of

\widetilde{}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[r]}
corresponds to
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}
.

The case
n=2
and
r=1
is of special interest. Then
K_{2}^{[1]}=\mathrm{U}(\Lambda_{0}^{\flat})
. It is equipped with a homomorphism

(6.1.4)

K_{2}^{[1]}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{1})^{2},

given by letting
K_{2}^{[1]}
act on the two one-dimensional hermitian spaces
(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})^{\vee}/\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}
and
\varpi^{-1}\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}/(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})^{\vee}
. This homomorphism is surjective, and the kernel is the pro-unipotent radical of
K_{2}^{[1]}
. The map induced in the generic fiber of
\widetilde{}\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
corresponds to the Galois cover given by the kernel of the first component of (
6.1.4
). Note that there is an involution
\theta
on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
, given by sending a point
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho)
to
(X^{\vee},\bar{\iota}^{\vee},\lambda^{\vee},(\rho^{\vee})^{-1})
. Here
\bar{\iota}^{\vee}\colon O_{F}\End(X^{\vee})
sends
a
to
\iota^{\vee}(\bar{a})
, and the polarization
\lambda^{\vee}
is the unique map
\lambda^{\vee}:X^{\vee}X
such that
\lambda^{\vee}\circ\lambda=\varpi\cdot\mathrm{id}
. Then
\theta
induces an involution in the generic fiber of
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
such that the induced action on
K_{2}^{[1]}
interchanges the two components of (
6.1.4
).

Now we define open compact subgroups on the general linear group side. We fix an orthogonal basis
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
of
W_{0}^{\flat}
and extend it by
u_{0}
(of length
\varpi^{\varepsilon}
) to an orthogonal basis of
W_{0}
; from now on we will use this basis to identify
\mathrm{GL}_{n,F}\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{F}(W^{\flat}_{0})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1,F}\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{F}(W_{0})
. We may assume that

(e_{i},e_{i})=\begin{cases}1,&1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
-\varpi^{\varepsilon},&i=n.\end{cases}

We may take
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}
to be the standard lattice with respect to the basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
. We define

(6.1.5)

K_{n}^{\prime}=\mathrm{GL}_{O_{F}}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})={\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}).

We also introduce
K_{n}^{\prime[r]}
, the joint stabilizer of
\Lambda^{\flat}
and its dual lattice
(\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee}
,

(6.1.6)

K_{n}^{\prime[r]}={\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}(\Lambda^{\flat})\cap{\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}((\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee}).

Then
K_{n}^{\prime[r]}
is a parahoric subgroup and
K_{n}^{\prime}
is a maximal parahoric subgroup.

Recall the self-dual lattice
\Lambda_{0}
chosen earlier at (
6.1.1
); it is related to
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}
through the following sequence of inclusions,

(6.1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle\subset^{1}\Lambda_{0}\subset^{1}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})^{\vee}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle^{\vee},&\text{ $r$ odd}\\
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle=\Lambda_{0}=(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})^{\vee}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle^{\vee},&\text{$r$ even.}\end{cases}

We define

(6.1.8)

K^{\prime}_{n+1}:=\mathrm{GL}_{O_{F}}(\Lambda_{0}),\quad K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}=\mathrm{GL}_{O_{F}}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle).

When
r
is even, then
K^{\prime}_{n+1}=K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}
. Also, we have an inclusion
K_{n}^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}
(but
K_{n}^{\prime}\not\subset K^{\prime}_{n+1}
if
r
is odd).
In the sequel, we consider the integral form of
G^{\prime}
defined by the lattice
(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})\oplus(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\oplus\langle{u_{0}}\rangle)
of
W_{0}^{\flat}\oplus W_{0}
, i.e.,
G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})=K^{\prime}_{n}\times K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}
.

We also define
the finite index subgroup
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}
of
K_{n}^{\prime[r]}
by

(6.1.9)

\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}:=K_{n}^{\prime[r]}\cap K^{\prime}_{n+1}={\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}(\Lambda^{\flat})\cap{\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)}((\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee})\cap{\rm Stab}_{\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)}(\Lambda_{0}).

More explicitly, the lattice
\Lambda_{0}
defines a lagrangian subspace inside the
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
-hermitian space
((\Lambda^{\flat})^{\vee}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle^{\vee})/(\Lambda^{\flat}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle)
of even dimension
r+\varepsilon
. Then
K_{n}^{\prime[r]}
acts on this hermitian space and
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}
is the stabilizer of this lagrangian subspace. In analogy to the unitary side (i.e., to
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}
), we expect that
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}
is not a parahoric subgroup when
r
is odd.

When defining orbital integrals, we need to choose Haar measures. For
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
we normalize the Haar measures by giving a maximal compact subgroup volume one. For
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
and
\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})(F_{0})
, we postulate

(6.1.10)

\vol(K_{n}^{\prime})=1,\quad\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[\varepsilon]})=1.

In other words, on the
\mathrm{GL}
-side, we normalize the Haar measures by giving the maximal compact subgroups volume one. On the
\mathrm{U}
-side, when

\varepsilon=0
, we normalize the Haar measures by giving the hyperspecial maximal compact subgroups volume one, whereas when
\varepsilon=1
, we normalize the Haar measures by giving the compact subgroup
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}
volume one.
We denote

(6.1.11)

\displaystyle c_{r}

\displaystyle:=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]})^{-1}=[\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[\varepsilon]}:\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}],

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{r}

\displaystyle:=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]})^{-1}=[K^{\prime}_{n}:\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}].

For
r=1
, we have
c_{1}=1
and
c_{1}^{\prime}=
the index in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
of the mirabolic subgroup (the subgroup of lower triangular matrices with
1
in the right lower corner). Hence
c_{1}^{\prime}=(q^{2}+1)(q^{2}-1)
.

6.2.
The case when
r
is odd

Consider the following element in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})

(6.2.1)

\displaystyle{\bf u}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{n}&\varpi^{-1}\,e_{n}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where
e_{n}=\,^{t}(0,\cdots,0,1)\in M_{n\times 1}(F_{0})
. It acts on
C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
by

({\bf u}\ast\phi^{\prime})(\gamma)=\phi^{\prime}({\bf u}^{-1}\gamma{\bf u}).

Set
h_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\varpi\cdot 1_{n}&\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
{\bf u^{\prime}}=h_{0}{\bf u}
. Then we have by (
6.1.7
) and the definitions (
6.1.8
) of
K^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}
,

(6.2.2)

K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}={\bf u^{\prime}}^{-1}K^{\prime}_{n+1}{\bf u^{\prime}}.

Define the function

(6.2.3)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{s}=(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\,{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}+((-1)^{n+1}q^{-(n+1)s}+1){\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})

and

(6.2.4)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}=\phi^{\prime}_{|{s=0}}.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

Let
r
be odd.

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
The function
\phi^{\prime}
is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)
.

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)
Let
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
be any function such that
\varphi^{\prime\natural}=c_{r}^{-1}\phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
. Then
\varphi^{\prime}
is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}},0)
.

Before giving the proof, we exhibit some functions as in the statement of Theorem
6.2.1
.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

Let
r
be odd.

(i)
Let
\varphi^{\prime}=\mathbf{1}_{K^{\prime}_{n}\times K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}=\mathbf{1}_{G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
. Then

\varphi^{\prime\natural}={\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}.

(ii) Let
\varphi^{\prime}=\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\times K^{\prime}_{n+1}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
. Then

\varphi^{\prime\natural}=c_{r}^{\prime-1}(-1)^{n}{\bf u^{\prime}}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}.

Proof.

(i) Recall from (
4.2.16
) the definition of
\varphi^{\prime\natural}
and note that
K^{\prime}_{n}\subset K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}
. The integral over
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
gives the function
\vol(K^{\prime}_{n}){\bf 1}_{K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}={\bf 1}_{K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)
. Then the integral over
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
gives the function
{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}
.

(ii) Similarly to (i), by
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\subset K^{\prime}_{n+1}
, the integral over
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
gives the function
\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}){\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}_{n+1}}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)
. To compute the second integral,
we note that
K^{\prime}_{n+1}={\bf u^{\prime}}K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}{\bf u^{\prime}}^{-1}
by (
6.2.2
). Now note that
{\bf u^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
. By (
4.2.16
), we may replace the function
{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}_{n+1}}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F)
by
\widetilde{\eta}^{n}({\bf u^{\prime}}){\bf 1}_{{\bf u^{\prime}}K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}=(-1)^{n^{2}}{\bf 1}_{{\bf u^{\prime}}K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}=(-1)^{n}{\bf 1}_{{\bf u^{\prime}}K^{\prime\dagger}_{n+1}}
and the assertion follows from the proof of part (i).
∎

Applying Theorem
6.2.1
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2.3
.

Let
r
be odd.
The following transfer statements hold.

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
The function

(6.2.5)

\phi^{\prime}_{r}=(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\,{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}+((-1)^{n+1}+1){\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})

of (
6.2.4
) is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)
.

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)
The function

(6.2.6)

\varphi^{\prime}_{r}=c_{r}\big(c_{r}^{\prime}(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\times K^{\prime}_{n+1}}+((-1)^{n+1}+1)\mathbf{1}_{G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})}\big)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})

is a transfer of
(c_{r}^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}},0)
.

Proof.

Part (i) is repeating part (i) of Theorem
6.2.1
. To show part (ii),
we first claim that
{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}
and
(-1)^{n}{\bf u^{\prime}}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}
have the same orbital integrals. In fact, by (
4.2.7
) we have

\Orb(\gamma,{\bf u^{\prime}}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})},s)=\omega_{S,s}(\gamma)\int_{H^{\prime}(F_{0})}{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}(h^{-1}_{0}h^{-1}\gamma hh_{0})\lvert\det h\rvert^{s}\eta(h)\,dh.

Since
h_{0}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
,
we may substitute
h
by
hh_{0}^{-1}
:

(6.2.7)

\displaystyle\Orb(\gamma,{\bf u^{\prime}}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})},s)=(-1)^{n}q^{ns}\Orb(\gamma,{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})},s).

Setting
s=0
proves the claim.
Now part (ii) follows from Lemma
6.2.2
and part (i).

∎

Remark 6.2.4
.

(i) In particular, when
n
is even, we see that the more natural looking function
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\times K^{\prime}_{n+1}}
is up to a scalar a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}},0)
. But when
n
is odd, this natural looking function does not seem to give the desired transfer.

(ii) The statement of Corollary
6.2.3
is the analogue of the FL which states in its homogeneous version that, when
u
has unit length, the function
\mathbf{1}_{{K}_{n}^{\prime}\times K^{\prime}_{n+1}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n}\times K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
. Its inhomogeneous version states that
\mathbf{1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}
is a transfer of
(\mathbf{1}_{K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}({W_{0}}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}({W_{1}}))
.

(iii) Let
r=1
. Then
c_{1}=1
, and the expression (
6.2.6
) of
\varphi^{\prime}_{1}
simplifies slightly. Also the function
\phi^{\prime}_{1}
of (
6.2.5
) transfers to
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)
. The case
r=1
of Corollary
6.2.3
is referred to in the sequel as the
quasi-canonical FL
. We restate it in Theorem
7.1.1
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
6.2.1
)
Part (ii) follows from part (i): we convert the homogeneous version for
f={\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})\times\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))
into the inhomogeneous version, cf. (
4.2.18
). Since
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\subset K_{n+1}
, it is easy to see that the resulting function is

f^{\natural}=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}){\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0})).

Therefore part (ii) follows from part (i) by Corollary
4.2.5
(cf. the end of §
4.2
).

We now prove part (i). We claim that we have an interpretation as lattice counting,

(6.2.8)

\displaystyle\Orb(g,{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}})=\#\{\Lambda\in{\mathrm{Vert}}^{0}(W_{0})\mid u_{0}\in\Lambda,g\Lambda=\Lambda\}.

Here
u_{0}\in W_{0}
is the special vector with valuation one. To show the claim, we note that the lattice
\Lambda_{0}^{\flat}:=\Lambda_{0}\cap W^{\flat}_{0}
is a vertex lattice of type
1
and
\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})(F_{0})\cap K_{n+1}=\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}
. By definition we have

\displaystyle\Orb(g,{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}}(h^{-1}gh)\,dh

\displaystyle=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]})\sum_{\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})/\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}}{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}}(h^{-1}gh).

The condition
h^{-1}gh\in K_{n+1}
is equivalent to

gh\Lambda_{0}=h\Lambda_{0}.

Let
\Xi
denote the set of lattices
\Lambda
of the form
h\Lambda_{0}
for
h\in\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})(F_{0})
such that
g\Lambda=\Lambda
, and let
\Xi^{\prime}
denote the set of lattices
\Lambda\in{\mathrm{Vert}}^{0}(W_{0})
such that
u_{0}\in\Lambda,g\Lambda=\Lambda
. Then clearly
\Xi\subset\Xi^{\prime}
. We now show the reverse inclusion. For
\Lambda\in\Xi^{\prime}
, we may write
\Lambda=h^{\prime}\Lambda_{0}
for some
h^{\prime}\in\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0})
. Since
u_{0}\in\Lambda
we have
h^{\prime-1}u_{0}\in\Lambda_{0}
. Note that
u_{0}
and
h^{\prime-1}u_{0}
are both in
\Lambda_{0}
and both of length
\varpi
. It follows that there exists
k\in K_{n+1}
such that
h^{\prime-1}u_{0}=k^{-1}u_{0}
(here we are using the fact that the compact open subgroup
K_{n+1}=\mathrm{U}(\Lambda_{0})
acts transitively on the set of length-
\varpi
vectors in
\Lambda_{0}
). Therefore
h:=h^{\prime}k^{-1}\in\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})
and
\Lambda=h^{\prime}\Lambda_{0}=h^{\prime}k^{-1}k\Lambda_{0}=h\Lambda_{0}\in\Xi
. We have thus proved
\Xi=\Xi^{\prime}
.
The claim now follows from
\sum_{\mathrm{U}(W^{\flat}_{0})/\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}}{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}}(h^{-1}gh)=\#\Xi=\#\Xi^{\prime}
, and
\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]})=1
by our normalization of measures.

Now we may relate (
6.2.8
) to the orbital integral in the semi-Lie algebra version, cf. (
4.2.14
),

\Orb(g,{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}})=\Orb((g,u_{0}),{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}\times\Lambda_{0}}),\quad g\in\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}.

Here on the right hand side the measure on
\mathrm{U}(W_{0})(F_{0})
is chosen such that
\vol(K_{n+1})=1
. Here we are implicitly using the relation between transfer factors, comp. (
6.2.12
) below.

As in (
4.1.8
), let
W^{\prime}=F_{0}^{n+1}\times(F_{0}^{n+1})^{\ast}
, and let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the standard lattice in
W^{\prime}(F_{0})
. Also let
K^{\prime}=S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})
. Then by the semi-Lie version of the Jacquet–Rallis FL, see Theorem
5.1.1
part
c
,
{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}\times\Lambda^{\prime}}
is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}\times\Lambda_{0}},0)
. Therefore we know that for regular semisimple
(\gamma,w)\in(S_{n+1}\times W^{\prime})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
matching
(g,u)\in(\mathrm{U}(W)\times W)(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
,

(6.2.9)

\displaystyle\Orb((\gamma,w),{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}\times\Lambda^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}\Orb((g,u),{\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}\times\Lambda_{0}}),&W=W_{0}\text{ split},\\
0,&W=W_{1}\text{ non-split}.\end{cases}

It suffices to relate the left hand side of (
6.2.9
) to the orbital integral (relative to
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
) of the function
\phi^{\prime}
defined by (
6.2.4
).
We may assume that
(g,u_{0})
matches
(\gamma,w_{0})
where
w_{0}
is the special vector

(6.2.10)

w_{0}=(\varpi\,e_{n+1},\,^{t}e_{n+1})\in W^{\prime}.

Then the assertion follows from the following lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.2.5
.

For all regular semisimple
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})
,

(6.2.11)

\Orb((\gamma,w_{0}),{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}\times\Lambda^{\prime}},s)=\Orb(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{s},s).

Here the RHS is defined by the formula (
4.2.7
), in which the function
\phi^{\prime}
has to be replaced by
\phi^{\prime}_{s}
.

Proof.

We first note that the transfer factors match

(6.2.12)

\omega_{S\times W^{\prime},s}(\gamma,w_{0})=\omega_{S,s}(\gamma)

(cf. (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.12
)).
Next we compare the integrals in (
4.2.7
) and (
4.2.11
).

By the Iwasawa decomposition, we may write

\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})\simeq Z\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})\times N(F_{0})\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}}),

where
Z\simeq F_{0}^{\times}
is the center,
N
(resp.
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
) is the unipotent radical (resp. the Levi) of the mirabolic subgroup. The Haar measure on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
can be taken as
the product measure of the Haar measures on the factors, normalized such that the natural maximal compact open subgroups all have volume one. Write an element in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
as a product
zhuk
according to the decomposition. Note that

{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}\times\Lambda^{\prime}}
is invariant under
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})
and hence the integral over
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})
can be dropped.
Then the integral in
\Orb((\gamma,w_{0}),{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}\times\Lambda^{\prime}},s)
(cf. (
4.2.11
)) decomposes into

\displaystyle\int_{F_{0}^{\times}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})\times N}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(u^{-1}h^{-1}\gamma hu){\bf 1}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(z^{-1}h^{-1}\varpi\,e_{n+1},\,^{t}e_{n+1}hu)\eta(h)\eta(z)^{n+1}|z|^{(n+1)s}|\det(h)|^{s}dz\,du\,dh.

Note that
h\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
acts trivially on the special vector
w_{0}=(\varpi\,e_{n+1},\,^{t}e_{n+1})
, and
{}^{t}e_{n+1}u=\,^{t}e_{n}
. Hence the condition

(u^{-1}h^{-1}z^{-1}\varpi\,e_{n+1},\,^{t}e_{n+1}zhu)\in\Lambda^{\prime}
is equivalent to

(u^{-1}z^{-1}\varpi\,e_{n+1},\,^{t}e_{n+1}z)\in\Lambda^{\prime}
. There are two cases.

(i)

{\rm val}(z)=0
. Then the integrality of
u^{-1}z^{-1}\varpi\,e_{n+1}
is equivalent to that of
\varpi u
.
The contribution to the orbital integral is the same as

(6.2.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})\times N(\varpi^{-1}O_{F_{0}})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(u^{-1}h^{-1}\gamma hu)\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh\,du.

Note that the integrand is
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F})
-invariant. Therefore we have for any
k\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F})
,

\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(ku^{-1}k^{-1}h^{-1}\gamma hkuk^{-1})\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(u^{-1}h^{-1}\gamma hu)\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh.

Note that we may identify
N
with
F_{0}^{n}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F_{0}})
acts on it in the standard way. The above invariance shows that the inner integral on
h\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
in (
6.2.13
), viewed as a function in
u\in N(F_{0})
, depends only on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F_{0}})
-orbit of
u
. There are precisely two
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(O_{F_{0}})
-orbits in
N(\varpi^{-1}O_{F_{0}})
, represented by
1
and the special element
{\bf u}
defined in (
6.2.1
).
It is now easy to see that the integral (
6.2.13
) is equal to

(6.2.14)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(h^{-1}\gamma h)\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh+(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}({\bf u}^{-1}h^{-1}\gamma h{\bf u})\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh.

(ii)

{\rm val}(z)=1
. Then
{\rm val}(z^{-1}\varpi)=0
. Hence by the integrality of
u^{-1}z^{-1}\varpi\,e_{n+1}
, we have
u\in\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})\cap N
. By the invariance of
{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}
under
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})
, this contribution to the orbital integral is the same as

(6.2.15)

\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}q^{-(n+1)s}\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})}{\bf 1}_{K^{\prime}}(h^{-1}\gamma h)\eta(h)|\det(h)|^{s}\,dh,

where the first factor is due to
\eta(z)^{n+1}|z|^{(n+1)s}=(-1)^{n+1}q^{-(n+1)s}
.

Combining (
6.2.14
) and (
6.2.15
), and using the equality (
6.2.12
) we obtain the required identity (
6.2.11
).
The proof is complete.
∎

6.3.
The case when
r
is even

In the previous subsection, we considered the case when
r
is odd. We state here the results in the case of even
r
, which is simpler. Now
\varepsilon=0
and
\langle u_{0}\rangle
is a direct summand:
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}\obot\langle u_{0}\rangle=\Lambda_{0}
,
where
\Lambda^{\flat}_{0}

is a self-dual lattice in
W_{0}^{\flat}
. In particular, we have

\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}:=K_{n}^{[r]}\cap\mathrm{U}(\Lambda_{0})=K_{n}^{[r]}\cap\mathrm{U}(\Lambda^{\flat}_{0})=K_{n}^{[0,r]}
,
cf. (
6.1.3
).

On the general linear group side, the two compact opens in (
6.1.8
) coincide and in (
6.1.9
) we have
\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}=K_{n}^{\prime[r]}
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

Let
r
be even. The following transfer statements hold.

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
The function

\phi_{r}^{\prime}={\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})

is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)
.

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)
The function

\varphi^{\prime}_{r}=\frac{c^{\prime}_{r}}{c_{r}}\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\times K_{n+1}^{\prime}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})

is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}},0)
.

Proof.

The proof of
part (i) is similar to that of Theorem
6.2.1
and we omit it. For part (ii), we have an analog of Lemma
6.2.2
:

(\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}\times K_{n+1}^{\prime}})^{{\natural}}=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[r]}){\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}

and

({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}})^{\natural}=\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}){\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}}.

Then the assertion follows from part (i).
∎

7.
The quasi-canonical FL and AFL

To state the quasi-canonical FL, resp. the quasi-canonical AFL, we assume that the special vectors
u_{0}\in W_{0}
, resp.
u_{1}\in W_{1}
, have length
\varpi
. The Haar measures on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
, on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
, on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(F_{0})
, and on
\mathrm{U}(W_{0}^{\flat})(F_{0})
are chosen as in (
6.1.10
) for
r=1
. Note that now we have
\vol(\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[1]})=1
.

7.1.
The quasi-canonical FL

The following theorem is just a restatement of Corollary
6.2.3
in the case
r=1
.

Theorem 7.1.1
.

(
Quasi-canonical FL
)
Let
p>2
.

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
The function

\phi^{\prime}=(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\,{\bf u}\ast{\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}+((-1)^{n+1}+1){\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})

is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1}))
.

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)
Recall that
c_{1}=1,c_{1}^{\prime}=(q^{2}+1)(q^{2}-1)
. The function

\varphi^{\prime}=c_{1}^{\prime}(q^{2(n+1)}-1)\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{\prime[1]}\times K^{\prime}_{n+1}}+((-1)^{n+1}+1)\mathbf{1}_{G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})

is a transfer of
({\bf 1}_{\tilde{K}_{n}^{[1]}\times K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
.∎

7.2.
The quasi-canonical AFL

We next turn to the quasi-canonical AFL.
We take up the setup in §
4
, with
n\geq 1
. We identify
W_{1}
with
\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
defined in §
3.2
in such a way that the special vector
u_{1}\in W_{1}
equals
{u}\in\mathbb{V}_{n+1}
defined in §
3.8
(assumed to have length
\varpi
). Then we may identify the hermitian space
W_{1}^{\flat}
with
\mathbb{W}_{n}^{[1]}
. Recall that
\mathcal{Z}(u)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
is the special divisor on
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
associated to
u
. Then
G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})
acts on
\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
via this identification and hence the arithmetic intersection number
\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),g\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}
defined in §
3.9
makes sense for
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})
.

Theorem 7.2.1
.

(
Quasi-canonical AFL
)

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
Let
\phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
be as in (i) of Theorem
7.1.1
. If
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
is matched with
g\in\mathrm{U}(W_{1})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, then

\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),(1,g)\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}\cdot\log q=-\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}\big)-\Orb\big(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\big),

where

\phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}=\begin{cases}(n+1){\bf 1}_{S_{n+1}(O_{F_{0}})}\cdot\log q,&$n$\text{ is even, }\\
0,&$n$\text{ is odd.}\end{cases}

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)

Let
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
as in (ii) of Theorem
7.1.1
. If
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
is matched with
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, then

\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),g\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}\cdot\log q=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}\big)-\Orb\big(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\big),

where

\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{c^{\prime}_{1}}(n+1){\bf 1}_{G^{\prime}(O_{F_{0}})}\cdot\log q,&$n$\text{ is even, }\\
0,&$n$\text{ is odd.}\end{cases}

The argument in
[
18
, Conj. 5.3, resp. Conj. 5.6, resp. Conj. 5.10]
of the implication of
a)\implies b)
in loc. cit. implies the following corollary.

Corollary 7.2.2
.

Assume the density conjecture
[
18
, Conj. 5.16]
.

(i)
(Inhomogeneous version)
Let
\phi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
be any transfer of
({\bf 1}_{K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{0}))\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathrm{U}(W_{1}))
. Then there exists
a function
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
such that, if
\gamma\in S_{n+1}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
is matched with
g\in\mathrm{U}(W_{1})(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, then

\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),(1\times g)\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}=-\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}\big)-\Orb\big(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\big).

(ii)
(Homogeneous version)
Let
\varphi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
be any transfer of
({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{K}_{n}^{[r]}\times K_{n+1}},0)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{0}})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{W_{1}})
. Then there exists
a function
\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})
such that, if
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
is matched with
g\in G_{W_{1}}(F_{0})_{\mathrm{rs}}
, then

\left\langle\mathcal{Z}(u),g\mathcal{Z}(u)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}}=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}\big)-\Orb\big(\gamma,\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{corr}}\big).

Proof.

We only give a sketch the proof as it is similar to that of
[
18
, Lemma 5.18]
assuming the density conjecture
[
18
, Conj. 5.16]
. Recall that the density conjecture of
loc. cit.
implies that the difference of two functions
\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi_{2}^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
with the same orbital integrals (for all regular semisimple orbits) must be of the form
\,{}^{\eta(h)h}\phi-\phi
for some
h\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{0})
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
, where
{}^{\eta(h)h}\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S_{n+1})
is the function
(^{\eta(h)h}\phi)(\gamma):=\eta(h)\phi(h^{-1}\gamma h)
. It is then straightforward to check that (cf.
[
18
, Lemma 5.12]
)

\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\phi^{\prime}_{1}-\phi_{2}^{\prime}\big)=\operatorname{\partial Orb}\big(\gamma,\,^{\eta(h)h}\phi-\phi)=c\Orb\big(\gamma,\phi)

for some constant
c
(independent of
\gamma
).
∎

Proof.

(of Theorem
7.2.1
)
Let us first prove the inhomogeneous version. We have a cartesian diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.5342pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.80675pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.33334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 31.81519pt\raise-15.83333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.60417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\square}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 40.53944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}(u)\times\mathcal{N}_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
